    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F60.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m Dr. ____. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Nice to meet you, Doctor. I came in because... well, it's my husband’s sister. She has been working this family business so she can get control of the money and push me out of all the decisions. And she thinks I don't notice! Just like last week, she came over bringing these cheap store-bought muffins. Who brings store-bought muffins when they know I bake? I've been baking since I was in middle school, right before I left school to help my mom out at home. I make a blueberry buckle that won a blue ribbon once at the county fair, you know. I use real butter, none of that margarine nonsense. But anyway, no matter how folks try to smooth it over and tell me she's just trying to be helpful, I know what I’ve been seeing for more than a year now. She wants the money and she wants me gone.\n\nDoctor: How certain are you that your husband’s sister is trying to get control of the money and push you out?\n\nPatient: Oh, I am one hundred percent certain. Like I was just saying, I know what I’ve been seeing for more than a year now. No matter how much folks try to smooth it over and tell me I'm just misunderstanding her, I know she's working this family business just so she can get control of the money and push me out of the decisions. \n\nIt's all about the money with her. Which reminds me, they handle a lot of cash at that shop, mostly hardware and small tools. I remember when my uncle used to run a little hardware store down in Georgia. We'd go visit every summer when I was a girl, and the heat down there... well, it's a wet heat, not like here. You step outside and you're just drenched in two minutes. My mom used to make us drink sweet tea by the gallon just to keep from passing out. She made the best sweet tea, always put a little sprig of mint in it from the garden. I tried growing mint in my own yard this past spring, but the soil just isn't right, or maybe the bugs get to it. The aphids were terrible this year on my roses, just ate them right up. I tried that soapy water trick but it didn't do a lick of good. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my sister-in-law. Yes, I'm completely sure of what she's up to. I'm not blind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices that other people could not hear?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never heard any voices. Well, except for the TV when I fall asleep on the couch, which happens more often than I'd like to admit. My husband always leaves the sports channel on, and those announcers just drone on and on. It's like a lullaby, really. Speaking of lullabies, I used to sing the sweetest little songs to my nieces when they were babies. They're all grown up now, of course, off to college and getting married. One of them just had a baby of her own, a little boy. They named him Oliver. Such an old-fashioned name, don't you think? I always liked the name Michael, myself. If I'd had a boy, I would have named him Michael. But we never had children. Just a house full of cats. We have three right now: Fluffy, Whiskers, and Barnaby. Barnaby is a terror, always knocking things off the counter. Just yesterday he broke my favorite coffee mug, the one I got in Florida... but no, no voices in my head, if that's what you mean. Just the cats meowing for their dinner.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any experiences of people sending you special messages or signs meant just for you?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't seen anything like that. Just regular things. Well, except for the mailman, he always leaves the letters kind of crooked in the box. I keep meaning to ask him about it, but he comes by so early. We used to have a mailman named Gary, oh, he was just wonderful. He'd always stop and give Barnaby a little scratch behind the ears if he was sitting on the porch. Gary retired last year, moved down to Arizona, I think. Or maybe it was New Mexico. Somewhere hot and dry. I couldn't live somewhere without trees. The changing of the leaves in the fall is my favorite time of year. We have this big oak tree in the front yard, and the acorns... oh my goodness, the acorns. Raking them up is a full-time job. My husband says we should just cut the tree down, but I couldn't bear it. It provides so much shade in the summer. But no, no special signs or anything like that. Just regular mail, mostly bills and those coupon flyers.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it is possible that your sister-in-law is not trying to control the money or push you out?\n\nPatient: No, absolutely not. I know what I've been seeing for over a year now. She's working that family business just so she can get her hands on all the money and push me right out of any decisions. My husband tries to smooth it over, says I'm just getting worked up over nothing, but I know better. He's always been like that, though, trying to keep the peace. Even when we were dating, he'd never send food back at a restaurant, even if it was stone cold. We used to go to this little diner on Main Street, they had the best cherry pie. Real cherries, not that canned filling stuff. The waitress there, Betty, she worked there for thirty years before they closed it down to build that new pharmacy. I don't know why we need another pharmacy, there's one on every corner now. And the prices... I went in just to get some aspirin and couldn't believe what they were charging. I ended up just going to the grocery store instead. The grocery store is always so crowded on Tuesdays, though, because of the senior discount. I'm not quite old enough for it yet, but my neighbor Mildred is. She always goes on Tuesdays and brings back those little bakery cookies... but no, I'm not wrong about my sister-in-law. I know exactly what she's doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way about other people being out to get you for as long as you can remember, even back in your younger adult years?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. It's just her, really. I never used to worry about people like this when I was younger. Back then, I was too busy working at the shoe factory. Oh, I worked there for twelve years before it shut down. We made the most beautiful leather boots. Real leather, you know, the kind that smells so rich when you open the box. I still have a pair I bought with my employee discount in 1990. They don't make shoes like that anymore, everything is plastic and falls apart after one season. It's a shame, really. The man who owned the factory, Mr. Henderson, he was such a fair boss. Every Christmas he'd give us all a turkey and a tin of those Danish butter cookies. The ones in the blue tin? I always ended up using the tin to store my sewing supplies. I used to sew a lot of my own clothes back then. Patterns were so cheap at the fabric store... but no, it's just my sister-in-law I have to keep an eye on now. I've always gotten along fine with everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally been suspicious of close friends or family members for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I've always been a pretty trusting person, really. I mean, my friends and I, we've known each other for years. My friend Susan, we met back when our kids were in kindergarten together. Her son, Tommy, he was the sweetest little boy, always had these bright red overalls on. They lived just two streets over, in that yellow house with the big wrap-around porch. I always wanted a porch like that. We used to sit out there in the summer and drink iced tea... real iced tea, not that powdered mix stuff. You have to boil the water and steep the bags just right, and add a sprig of mint if you have it. I tried growing mint once, but it just took over the whole garden. It choked out all my tomatoes. I was so upset, I had been planning on canning those tomatoes. My grandmother taught me how to can when I was just a little girl. We'd spend all day in the hot kitchen, putting up peaches and green beans... but no, I've never had any trouble with my friends or my own side of the family. It's just her.\n\nDoctor: Besides your sister-in-law, do you also tend to distrust other people’s motives in general?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't think so. I mean, I've never been the type to think everyone is out to get me. I've always taken people at their word, for the most part. Like the mailman, Gary. He's been delivering our mail for fifteen years, maybe more. He always makes sure to put the packages right inside the screen door so they don't get wet if it rains. We had this awful storm last spring, the wind was howling so loud it sounded like a freight train coming right through the living room. It knocked down that big oak tree in the Johnsons' yard across the street. Took out their whole front fence and crushed their birdbath. It was a beautiful birdbath, too, made of real stone, not that cheap concrete they sell at the garden center now. I used to love watching the robins splash around in it in the mornings while I drank my coffee. I only drink one cup a day now, though, doctor said it was better for my stomach. But anyway... no, I don't distrust people in general. I just know what I see with her.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought that your husband might be involved in this too?\n\nPatient: No, no, definitely not my husband. He's a good man, he really is. We've been married for almost thirty years now. We met at a county fair, actually. He was working one of those games where you try to knock down the milk bottles with a baseball. You know the ones? They always rig them so the bottom bottles are heavier, it's almost impossible to win the giant stuffed bear. But he let me win a little blue dog anyway. I still have it, sitting on the dresser in the spare room. We painted that room a soft yellow last year... well, he painted it. I picked out the color, 'Morning Sunshine' it was called. It took three coats to cover up the awful green that was there before. We bought the paint at that hardware store downtown, the one with the creaky wooden floors. I love the smell of that place, sawdust and fertilizer. The owner, old Mr. Higgins, he just retired last month. His son took over, but it's not the same. The son doesn't know anything about plumbing fixtures... but no, my husband doesn't have anything to do with what she's doing. He just doesn't see it like I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things that other people could not see?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I mean, my eyesight isn't what it used to be, I'll admit that. I had to get bifocals last year. I fought it for the longest time, I really did. I thought they would make me look old, you know? Like my Aunt Clara. She had those thick, heavy frames with the little chain attached so she wouldn't lose them. She was always losing things, though. Once she lost her false teeth at a church picnic. We spent an hour looking for them, and it turned out she had wrapped them in a napkin and put them in her purse next to a half-eaten deviled egg. Deviled eggs always give me heartburn now, which is a shame because I make a wonderful deviled egg. The secret is just a tiny pinch of dry mustard and a splash of pickle juice. Sweet pickle juice, not dill. Dill is too sharp. I tried making dill pickles once, grew the cucumbers myself on a trellis by the back fence. But the rabbits kept getting to them before they were big enough to pick. Rabbits are cute, but they're a menace to a garden... but no, I don't see things that aren't there. My eyes are fine, except for needing the glasses for reading.\n\nDoctor: Do you generally prefer to be alone rather than spend time with other people?\n\nPatient: Oh, I wouldn't say I prefer to be alone. I like having company just fine. I used to host a bunco night with some ladies from the neighborhood a few years back. We'd take turns hosting, and whenever it was my turn, I'd make these little cucumber sandwiches. You have to cut the crusts off, or they just don't look right on the platter. I had this beautiful silver platter that belonged to my mother. She got it as a wedding gift, and she kept it polished so bright you could see your face in it. She used this special pink polish that came in a little tin. I can't find that brand anywhere anymore, I've looked all over. I even asked the boy at the grocery store last week, the one with the curly hair who always bags the groceries too heavy. He put all my canned peaches in one paper bag, and the bottom ripped right before I got to the car. Peaches rolling all over the asphalt. Luckily they were in cans, not glass jars, or it would have been a terrible, sticky mess. I used to can my own peaches, actually, back when we had a tree in the yard. But no, I don't mind being around people at all. I just like things peaceful, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you actually want close relationships with other people, or are you generally content keeping to yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, I do want close relationships, I really do. I've always been a friendly person. I mean, I used to have coffee with my neighbor, Mrs. Gable, every Tuesday morning. She lived in the blue house at the end of the cul-de-sac. It was a lovely shade of blue, kind of a robin's egg color. She had this beautiful rose garden in her front yard, the kind with those big, showy tea roses. I tried growing roses once, but the Japanese beetles just decimated them. They are awful little bugs, so shiny and green, but they just eat everything in sight. I bought this special spray from the garden center, but I think it just made them mad. The man at the garden center, he had this booming voice, you could hear him from the parking lot. He told me I needed to put out these traps, little bags that hang from a stand. But then I read somewhere that the traps actually attract *more* beetles to your yard, so I threw them all in the trash. The trash men come so early on our street, sometimes before the sun is even up. It wakes me up, the sound of that big truck grinding... but yes, I like having people close to me. I just don't have as many friends as I used to, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever, at any point earlier in your life, had similar suspicions that people were trying to use, deceive, or harm you?\n\nPatient: No, not like this. Years ago, she borrowed my good crockpot for a church potluck and she didn't return it for three months. And when she finally did, the ceramic insert had a chip right on the rim. She swore it was there before, but I know my own kitchenware. I take very good care of my things. I always hand-wash the insert, never put it in the dishwasher, because the dishwasher detergent can be so harsh. It leaves that chalky residue on the glasses, too. I started using that rinse aid, the blue liquid in the little bottle, and it helped some, but you have to remember to refill the little compartment in the door. The door on my dishwasher is getting kind of squeaky, actually. I probably need to spray some WD-40 on the hinges. WD-40 fixes just about everything, doesn't it? My father used to keep a can of it in his glove compartment, right next to his road flares and this old, folded-up paper map. People don't use paper maps anymore, with the phones doing all the navigating. But I always liked the maps, tracing the lines with your finger... but no, I've never felt like people were plotting against me or trying to ruin me before. It's really just Brenda. I mean, I'm a trusting person, usually. I trust the mailman to bring the mail, don't I? Although, lately, he's been coming much later in the afternoon. Used to be he'd come right at 11:30, regular as clockwork. I could set my watch by it. Now sometimes it's past 3:00 before I hear the little squeak of the mailbox hinge. Our mailbox is one of those old metal ones, shaped like a little barn. My husband painted it red when we first moved in, but the sun has faded it to a sort of dusty pink. I was thinking of repainting it, maybe a nice hunter green. Green is such a calming color, don't you think? Like the forest. I went camping in the forest once when I was a teenager, up near the state park. We roasted marshmallows and I burned my tongue because I didn't wait for it to cool down. It blistered right up. But anyway, no, I don't go around suspecting everyone. Just Brenda, because of... well, you know how she is.\n\nDoctor: Have you usually been this suspicious or mistrustful of other people for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not at all. I started working at the diner over on Route 9 when I was sixteen—I actually left high school early because the tips were so good, and I just trusted folks to leave a fair amount on the table. It's really just been recently, with Brenda.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this kind of mistrust toward people other than your sister-in-law?\n\nPatient: No, like I said, it's just Brenda. They're so scratchy, though, I always have to wear a thin cotton pair underneath or my ankles break out in a rash. I get contact dermatitis really easily, even from certain laundry detergents. I can only use the clear, unscented kind now. If I use anything with a fragrance, I'm itching for a week. But yes, no matter what anyone says, I know exactly what Brenda is up to with the business and the money.\n\nDoctor: Besides your sister-in-law, do you ever suspect that other people are trying to use or deceive you?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I really don't. Like I said, it's just Brenda who has it out for me with the money. Most people are perfectly fine. I mean, my neighbor across the street, Mrs. Higgins, she's a sweetheart. She brought over a casserole last week when I was feeling a bit under the weather. It was a tuna noodle casserole, the kind with the crushed potato chips on top? I haven't had that since I was a little girl. My grandmother used to make it every Friday during Lent because we couldn't eat meat. She'd use those really wide egg noodles, and she always put peas in it, which I hated. I used to pick the peas out and hide them under my napkin. Once, my grandfather caught me doing it, but he just winked and didn't say a word. He was a good man, worked at the lumber mill for forty years until he lost two fingers in a bandsaw accident. He had to learn how to hold his coffee mug with his left hand after that... but no, nobody else is trying to use me or anything like that. Just Brenda.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Nice to meet you, Doctor. I came in because... well, it's my husband’s sister. She has been working this family business so she can get control of the money and push me out of all the decisions. And she thinks I don't notice! But anyway, no matter how folks try to smooth it over and tell me she's just trying to be helpful, I know what I’ve been seeing for more than a year now. No matter how much folks try to smooth it over and tell me I'm just misunderstanding her, I know she's working this family business just so she can get control of the money and push me out of the decisions. \n\nIt's all about the money with her. It's like a lullaby, really. Speaking of lullabies, I used to sing the sweetest little songs to my nieces when they were babies. They're all grown up now, of course, off to college and getting married. One of them just had a baby of her own, a little boy. They named him Oliver. Such an old-fashioned name, don't you think? I always liked the name Michael, myself. If I'd had a boy, I would have named him Michael. But we never had children. Just a house full of cats. We have three right now: Fluffy, Whiskers, and Barnaby. Barnaby is a terror, always knocking things off the counter. Just regular things. Well, except for the mailman, he always leaves the letters kind of crooked in the box. I keep meaning to ask him about it, but he comes by so early. We used to have a mailman named Gary, oh, he was just wonderful. He'd always stop and give Barnaby a little scratch behind the ears if he was sitting on the porch. Gary retired last year, moved down to Arizona, I think. Or maybe it was New Mexico. Somewhere hot and dry. I couldn't live somewhere without trees. The changing of the leaves in the fall is my favorite time of year. We have this big oak tree in the front yard, and the acorns... oh my goodness, the acorns. Raking them up is a full-time job. My husband says we should just cut the tree down, but I couldn't bear it. It provides so much shade in the summer. But no, no special signs or anything like that. Just regular mail, mostly bills and those coupon flyers.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it is possible that your sister-in-law is not trying to control the money or push you out?\n\nPatient: No, absolutely not. Real cherries, not that canned filling stuff. The waitress there, Betty, she worked there for thirty years before they closed it down to build that new pharmacy. I don't know why we need another pharmacy, there's one on every corner now. And the prices... I went in just to get some aspirin and couldn't believe what they were charging. I ended up just going to the grocery store instead. The grocery store is always so crowded on Tuesdays, though, because of the senior discount. I'm not quite old enough for it yet, but my neighbor Mildred is. She always goes on Tuesdays and brings back those little bakery cookies... but no, I'm not wrong about my sister-in-law. I know exactly what she's doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way about other people being out to get you for as long as you can remember, even back in your younger adult years?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. It's just her, really. I never used to worry about people like this when I was younger. Back then, I was too busy working at the shoe factory. Oh, I worked there for twelve years before it shut down. We made the most beautiful leather boots. Real leather, you know, the kind that smells so rich when you open the box. I still have a pair I bought with my employee discount in 1990. They don't make shoes like that anymore, everything is plastic and falls apart after one season. It's a shame, really. The man who owned the factory, Mr. Henderson, he was such a fair boss. Every Christmas he'd give us all a turkey and a tin of those Danish butter cookies. The ones in the blue tin? I always ended up using the tin to store my sewing supplies. I used to sew a lot of my own clothes back then. Patterns were so cheap at the fabric store... but no, it's just my sister-in-law I have to keep an eye on now. I've always gotten along fine with everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally been suspicious of close friends or family members for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I've always been a pretty trusting person, really. I mean, my friends and I, we've known each other for years. My friend Susan, we met back when our kids were in kindergarten together. Her son, Tommy, he was the sweetest little boy, always had these bright red overalls on. They lived just two streets over, in that yellow house with the big wrap-around porch. I always wanted a porch like that. We used to sit out there in the summer and drink iced tea... real iced tea, not that powdered mix stuff. You have to boil the water and steep the bags just right, and add a sprig of mint if you have it. I tried growing mint once, but it just took over the whole garden. It choked out all my tomatoes. I was so upset, I had been planning on canning those tomatoes. My grandmother taught me how to can when I was just a little girl. We'd spend all day in the hot kitchen, putting up peaches and green beans... but no, I've never had any trouble with my friends or my own side of the family. It's just her.\n\nDoctor: Besides your sister-in-law, do you also tend to distrust other people’s motives in general?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't think so. I mean, I've never been the type to think everyone is out to get me. I've always taken people at their word, for the most part. Like the mailman, Gary. He's been delivering our mail for fifteen years, maybe more. He always makes sure to put the packages right inside the screen door so they don't get wet if it rains. We had this awful storm last spring, the wind was howling so loud it sounded like a freight train coming right through the living room. It knocked down that big oak tree in the Johnsons' yard across the street. Took out their whole front fence and crushed their birdbath. It was a beautiful birdbath, too, made of real stone, not that cheap concrete they sell at the garden center now. But anyway... no, I don't distrust people in general. They always rig them so the bottom bottles are heavier, it's almost impossible to win the giant stuffed bear. But he let me win a little blue dog anyway. I've always been a friendly person. I mean, I used to have coffee with my neighbor, Mrs. Gable, every Tuesday morning. She lived in the blue house at the end of the cul-de-sac. I bought this special spray from the garden center, but I think it just made them mad. The man at the garden center, he had this booming voice, you could hear him from the parking lot. The trash men come so early on our street, sometimes before the sun is even up. It wakes me up, the sound of that big truck grinding... but yes, I like having people close to me. I just don't have as many friends as I used to, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever, at any point earlier in your life, had similar suspicions that people were trying to use, deceive, or harm you?\n\nPatient: No, not like this. I mean, people have always been a little selfish, haven't they? That's just human nature. Like my sister-in-law, Brenda. Years ago, she borrowed my good crockpot for a church potluck and she didn't return it for three months. And when she finally did, the ceramic insert had a chip right on the rim. She swore it was there before, but I know my own kitchenware. I take very good care of my things. I always hand-wash the insert, never put it in the dishwasher, because the dishwasher detergent can be so harsh. It leaves that chalky residue on the glasses, too. I started using that rinse aid, the blue liquid in the little bottle, and it helped some, but you have to remember to refill the little compartment in the door. The door on my dishwasher is getting kind of squeaky, actually. I probably need to spray some WD-40 on the hinges. WD-40 fixes just about everything, doesn't it? My father used to keep a can of it in his glove compartment, right next to his road flares and this old, folded-up paper map. People don't use paper maps anymore, with the phones doing all the navigating. But I always liked the maps, tracing the lines with your finger... but no, I've never felt like people were plotting against me or trying to ruin me before. Used to be he'd come right at 11:30, regular as clockwork. I could set my watch by it. Now sometimes it's past 3:00 before I hear the little squeak of the mailbox hinge. Our mailbox is one of those old metal ones, shaped like a little barn. My husband painted it red when we first moved in, but the sun has faded it to a sort of dusty pink. I was thinking of repainting it, maybe a nice hunter green. Green is such a calming color, don't you think? Like the forest. I went camping in the forest once when I was a teenager, up near the state park. We roasted marshmallows and I burned my tongue because I didn't wait for it to cool down. It blistered right up. But anyway, no, I don't go around suspecting everyone. Just Brenda, because of... well, you know how she is.\n\nDoctor: Have you usually been this suspicious or mistrustful of other people for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not at all. I've always been pretty open with people, ever since I was young. I started working at the diner over on Route 9 when I was sixteen—I actually left high school early because the tips were so good, and I just trusted folks to leave a fair amount on the table. We had these little glass sugar dispensers at every booth, the kind with the little metal flap on the lid? You had to unscrew them every single night and wipe them out because the sugar would get all clumpy if it was humid. The humidity here in the summer is just dreadful, isn't it? It makes my hair frizz up terribly. I bought this special smoothing serum from the drugstore, it comes in a little purple pump bottle, but it just made my hair look greasy instead of smooth. My mother always used to say a woman's hair is her crowning glory, but she wore a wig for the last twenty years of her life. It was a very stiff wig, sort of an auburn color, and she kept it on a styrofoam head on her dresser... but, oh, what were we talking about? Right, being mistrustful. No, I've never been that way. It's really just been recently, with Brenda.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this kind of mistrust toward people other than your sister-in-law?\n\nPatient: No, like I said, it's just Brenda. I don't have any reason to doubt anyone else. Well, except maybe the guy at the auto shop, but that's just because mechanics always try to upcharge you for things you don't need, like air filters. He showed me this dirty filter last time I went in for an oil change, but I'm pretty sure he just keeps one dirty filter in the back to show all the ladies who come in by themselves. My husband used to do all the oil changes in the driveway. He had these heavy metal ramps he'd drive the car up onto. They were so heavy, I could barely lift one end of them. They got rust spots all over them because he left them out in the rain one spring. It rained for almost two weeks straight that year, my tomato plants completely drowned in the garden. I had planted these beautiful heirloom tomatoes, the big purple ones? They're so sweet if you slice them up with a little mozzarella and balsamic vinegar. I buy the balsamic glaze, the thick kind in the squeeze bottle, it's so much better than the thin stuff... but anyway, no, I don't mistrust people generally. Just Brenda.\n\nDoctor: Has Brenda ever actually done anything concrete that you could point to as evidence that she is trying to control the money, or is it mainly a strong feeling you have about her intentions?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not just a feeling, I know what I've been seeing for more than a year now. No matter how much folks try to smooth it over and tell me I'm just misunderstanding her, I know she's working this family business just so she can get control of the money and push me out of the decisions. \n\nIt's all about the money with her. We used to have a mailman named Gary, oh, he was just wonderful. He'd always stop and give Barnaby a little scratch behind the ears if he was sitting on the porch. Gary retired last year, moved down to Arizona, I think. Or maybe it was New Mexico. Somewhere hot and dry. I couldn't live somewhere without trees. The changing of the leaves in the fall is my favorite time of year. We have this big oak tree in the front yard, and the acorns... oh my goodness, the acorns. Raking them up is a full-time job. My husband says we should just cut the tree down, but I couldn't bear it. It provides so much shade in the summer. But no, no special signs or anything like that. Just regular mail, mostly bills and those coupon flyers.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it is possible that your sister-in-law is not trying to control the money or push you out?\n\nPatient: No, absolutely not. Even when we were dating, he'd never send food back at a restaurant, even if it was stone cold. We used to go to this little diner on Main Street, they had the best cherry pie. Real cherries, not that canned filling stuff. The waitress there, Betty, she worked there for thirty years before they closed it down to build that new pharmacy. I don't know why we need another pharmacy, there's one on every corner now. And the prices... I went in just to get some aspirin and couldn't believe what they were charging. I ended up just going to the grocery store instead. The grocery store is always so crowded on Tuesdays, though, because of the senior discount. I'm not quite old enough for it yet, but my neighbor Mildred is. She always goes on Tuesdays and brings back those little bakery cookies... but no, I'm not wrong about my sister-in-law. It's just her, really. I never used to worry about people like this when I was younger. Back then, I was too busy working at the shoe factory. Oh, I worked there for twelve years before it shut down. We made the most beautiful leather boots. Real leather, you know, the kind that smells so rich when you open the box. I still have a pair I bought with my employee discount in 1990. They don't make shoes like that anymore, everything is plastic and falls apart after one season. It's a shame, really. The man who owned the factory, Mr. Henderson, he was such a fair boss. Every Christmas he'd give us all a turkey and a tin of those Danish butter cookies. The ones in the blue tin? I always ended up using the tin to store my sewing supplies. I used to sew a lot of my own clothes back then. Patterns were so cheap at the fabric store... but no, it's just my sister-in-law I have to keep an eye on now. I've always gotten along fine with everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally been suspicious of close friends or family members for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I've always been a pretty trusting person, really. I mean, my friends and I, we've known each other for years. My friend Susan, we met back when our kids were in kindergarten together. Her son, Tommy, he was the sweetest little boy, always had these bright red overalls on. They lived just two streets over, in that yellow house with the big wrap-around porch. I always wanted a porch like that. We used to sit out there in the summer and drink iced tea... real iced tea, not that powdered mix stuff. You have to boil the water and steep the bags just right, and add a sprig of mint if you have it. I tried growing mint once, but it just took over the whole garden. It choked out all my tomatoes. I was so upset, I had been planning on canning those tomatoes. My grandmother taught me how to can when I was just a little girl. We'd spend all day in the hot kitchen, putting up peaches and green beans... but no, I've never had any trouble with my friends or my own side of the family. It's just her.\n\nDoctor: Besides your sister-in-law, do you also tend to distrust other people’s motives in general?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't think so. I mean, I've never been the type to think everyone is out to get me. I've always taken people at their word, for the most part. Like the mailman, Gary. He's been delivering our mail for fifteen years, maybe more. He always makes sure to put the packages right inside the screen door so they don't get wet if it rains. We had this awful storm last spring, the wind was howling so loud it sounded like a freight train coming right through the living room. It knocked down that big oak tree in the Johnsons' yard across the street. Took out their whole front fence and crushed their birdbath. It was a beautiful birdbath, too, made of real stone, not that cheap concrete they sell at the garden center now. But anyway... no, I don't distrust people in general. We've been married for almost thirty years now. We met at a county fair, actually. He was working one of those games where you try to knock down the milk bottles with a baseball. You know the ones? They always rig them so the bottom bottles are heavier, it's almost impossible to win the giant stuffed bear. But he let me win a little blue dog anyway. It took three coats to cover up the awful green that was there before. We bought the paint at that hardware store downtown, the one with the creaky wooden floors. I love the smell of that place, sawdust and fertilizer. The owner, old Mr. Higgins, he just retired last month. His son took over, but it's not the same. The son doesn't know anything about plumbing fixtures... but no, my husband doesn't have anything to do with what she's doing. He just doesn't see it like I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things that other people could not see?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I mean, my eyesight isn't what it used to be, I'll admit that. She had those thick, heavy frames with the little chain attached so she wouldn't lose them. She was always losing things, though. Once she lost her false teeth at a church picnic. We spent an hour looking for them, and it turned out she had wrapped them in a napkin and put them in her purse next to a half-eaten deviled egg. Sweet pickle juice, not dill. Dill is too sharp. I tried making dill pickles once, grew the cucumbers myself on a trellis by the back fence. My eyes are fine, except for needing the glasses for reading.\n\nDoctor: Do you generally prefer to be alone rather than spend time with other people?\n\nPatient: Oh, I wouldn't say I prefer to be alone. I like having company just fine. I used to host a bunco night with some ladies from the neighborhood a few years back. We'd take turns hosting, and whenever it was my turn, I'd make these little cucumber sandwiches. You have to cut the crusts off, or they just don't look right on the platter. I had this beautiful silver platter that belonged to my mother. She got it as a wedding gift, and she kept it polished so bright you could see your face in it. She used this special pink polish that came in a little tin. I can't find that brand anywhere anymore, I've looked all over. I even asked the boy at the grocery store last week, the one with the curly hair who always bags the groceries too heavy. He put all my canned peaches in one paper bag, and the bottom ripped right before I got to the car. Peaches rolling all over the asphalt. Luckily they were in cans, not glass jars, or it would have been a terrible, sticky mess. I used to can my own peaches, actually, back when we had a tree in the yard. But no, I don't mind being around people at all. I just like things peaceful, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you actually want close relationships with other people, or are you generally content keeping to yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, I do want close relationships, I really do. I've always been a friendly person. I mean, I used to have coffee with my neighbor, Mrs. Gable, every Tuesday morning. She lived in the blue house at the end of the cul-de-sac. It was a lovely shade of blue, kind of a robin's egg color. She had this beautiful rose garden in her front yard, the kind with those big, showy tea roses. I tried growing roses once, but the Japanese beetles just decimated them. They are awful little bugs, so shiny and green, but they just eat everything in sight. I bought this special spray from the garden center, but I think it just made them mad. The man at the garden center, he had this booming voice, you could hear him from the parking lot. Our mailbox is one of those old metal ones, shaped like a little barn. My husband painted it red when we first moved in, but the sun has faded it to a sort of dusty pink. I've always been pretty open with people, ever since I was young. I started working at the diner over on Route 9 when I was sixteen—I actually left high school early because the tips were so good, and I just trusted folks to leave a fair amount on the table. We had these little glass sugar dispensers at every booth, the kind with the little metal flap on the lid? You had to unscrew them every single night and wipe them out because the sugar would get all clumpy if it was humid. The humidity here in the summer is just dreadful, isn't it? It makes my hair frizz up terribly. I bought this special smoothing serum from the drugstore, it comes in a little purple pump bottle, but it just made my hair look greasy instead of smooth. My mother always used to say a woman's hair is her crowning glory, but she wore a wig for the last twenty years of her life. It was a very stiff wig, sort of an auburn color, and she kept it on a styrofoam head on her dresser... but, oh, what were we talking about? Right, being mistrustful. No, I've never been that way. It's really just been recently, with Brenda.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this kind of mistrust toward people other than your sister-in-law?\n\nPatient: No, like I said, it's just Brenda. Just Brenda.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least a week where you felt unusually high, very energetic, or needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't ever felt like that. It's a little terrier mix, scruffy looking thing, and they leave him out on the patio all night. I don't know why people get dogs if they're just going to leave them outside in the dark. It gets so chilly out there, especially in October. I bought this heavy quilt at a craft fair last October, actually. It has these beautiful patchwork squares, all done in autumn colors—burnt orange, mustard yellow, a really deep burgundy. The lady who made it said it took her six months to stitch by hand. She was from two towns over, said she learned to quilt from her mother when she was just a little girl. I used to sew a bit, mostly just mending things, hemming pants. My husband was always tearing the knees out of his work jeans. I bought these iron-on patches once, but they never stayed on after you washed them. You'd pull them out of the dryer and the patch would just be flapping by one corner... but no, I've never had a week where I felt high or didn't need sleep.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF22, F60.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m Dr. ____. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Nice to meet you, Doctor. I came in because... well, it's my husband’s sister. She has been working this family business so she can get control of the money and push me out of all the decisions. And she thinks I don't notice! Just like last week, she came over bringing these cheap store-bought muffins. Who brings store-bought muffins when they know I bake? I've been baking since I was in middle school, right before I left school to help my mom out at home. I make a blueberry buckle that won a blue ribbon once at the county fair, you know. I use real butter, none of that margarine nonsense. But anyway, no matter how folks try to smooth it over and tell me she's just trying to be helpful, I know what I’ve been seeing for more than a year now. She wants the money and she wants me gone.\n\nDoctor: How certain are you that your husband’s sister is trying to get control of the money and push you out?\n\nPatient: Oh, I am one hundred percent certain. Like I was just saying, I know what I’ve been seeing for more than a year now. No matter how much folks try to smooth it over and tell me I'm just misunderstanding her, I know she's working this family business just so she can get control of the money and push me out of the decisions. \n\nIt's all about the money with her. Which reminds me, they handle a lot of cash at that shop, mostly hardware and small tools. I remember when my uncle used to run a little hardware store down in Georgia. We'd go visit every summer when I was a girl, and the heat down there... well, it's a wet heat, not like here. You step outside and you're just drenched in two minutes. My mom used to make us drink sweet tea by the gallon just to keep from passing out. She made the best sweet tea, always put a little sprig of mint in it from the garden. I tried growing mint in my own yard this past spring, but the soil just isn't right, or maybe the bugs get to it. The aphids were terrible this year on my roses, just ate them right up. I tried that soapy water trick but it didn't do a lick of good. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my sister-in-law. Yes, I'm completely sure of what she's up to. I'm not blind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices that other people could not hear?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never heard any voices. Well, except for the TV when I fall asleep on the couch, which happens more often than I'd like to admit. My husband always leaves the sports channel on, and those announcers just drone on and on. It's like a lullaby, really. Speaking of lullabies, I used to sing the sweetest little songs to my nieces when they were babies. They're all grown up now, of course, off to college and getting married. One of them just had a baby of her own, a little boy. They named him Oliver. Such an old-fashioned name, don't you think? I always liked the name Michael, myself. If I'd had a boy, I would have named him Michael. But we never had children. Just a house full of cats. We have three right now: Fluffy, Whiskers, and Barnaby. Barnaby is a terror, always knocking things off the counter. Just yesterday he broke my favorite coffee mug, the one I got in Florida... but no, no voices in my head, if that's what you mean. Just the cats meowing for their dinner.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any experiences of people sending you special messages or signs meant just for you?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't seen anything like that. Just regular things. Well, except for the mailman, he always leaves the letters kind of crooked in the box. I keep meaning to ask him about it, but he comes by so early. We used to have a mailman named Gary, oh, he was just wonderful. He'd always stop and give Barnaby a little scratch behind the ears if he was sitting on the porch. Gary retired last year, moved down to Arizona, I think. Or maybe it was New Mexico. Somewhere hot and dry. I couldn't live somewhere without trees. The changing of the leaves in the fall is my favorite time of year. We have this big oak tree in the front yard, and the acorns... oh my goodness, the acorns. Raking them up is a full-time job. My husband says we should just cut the tree down, but I couldn't bear it. It provides so much shade in the summer. But no, no special signs or anything like that. Just regular mail, mostly bills and those coupon flyers.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it is possible that your sister-in-law is not trying to control the money or push you out?\n\nPatient: No, absolutely not. I know what I've been seeing for over a year now. She's working that family business just so she can get her hands on all the money and push me right out of any decisions. My husband tries to smooth it over, says I'm just getting worked up over nothing, but I know better. He's always been like that, though, trying to keep the peace. Even when we were dating, he'd never send food back at a restaurant, even if it was stone cold. We used to go to this little diner on Main Street, they had the best cherry pie. Real cherries, not that canned filling stuff. The waitress there, Betty, she worked there for thirty years before they closed it down to build that new pharmacy. I don't know why we need another pharmacy, there's one on every corner now. And the prices... I went in just to get some aspirin and couldn't believe what they were charging. I ended up just going to the grocery store instead. The grocery store is always so crowded on Tuesdays, though, because of the senior discount. I'm not quite old enough for it yet, but my neighbor Mildred is. She always goes on Tuesdays and brings back those little bakery cookies... but no, I'm not wrong about my sister-in-law. I know exactly what she's doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way about other people being out to get you for as long as you can remember, even back in your younger adult years?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. It's just her, really. I never used to worry about people like this when I was younger. Back then, I was too busy working at the shoe factory. Oh, I worked there for twelve years before it shut down. We made the most beautiful leather boots. Real leather, you know, the kind that smells so rich when you open the box. I still have a pair I bought with my employee discount in 1990. They don't make shoes like that anymore, everything is plastic and falls apart after one season. It's a shame, really. The man who owned the factory, Mr. Henderson, he was such a fair boss. Every Christmas he'd give us all a turkey and a tin of those Danish butter cookies. The ones in the blue tin? I always ended up using the tin to store my sewing supplies. I used to sew a lot of my own clothes back then. Patterns were so cheap at the fabric store... but no, it's just my sister-in-law I have to keep an eye on now. I've always gotten along fine with everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally been suspicious of close friends or family members for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I've always been a pretty trusting person, really. I mean, my friends and I, we've known each other for years. My friend Susan, we met back when our kids were in kindergarten together. Her son, Tommy, he was the sweetest little boy, always had these bright red overalls on. They lived just two streets over, in that yellow house with the big wrap-around porch. I always wanted a porch like that. We used to sit out there in the summer and drink iced tea... real iced tea, not that powdered mix stuff. You have to boil the water and steep the bags just right, and add a sprig of mint if you have it. I tried growing mint once, but it just took over the whole garden. It choked out all my tomatoes. I was so upset, I had been planning on canning those tomatoes. My grandmother taught me how to can when I was just a little girl. We'd spend all day in the hot kitchen, putting up peaches and green beans... but no, I've never had any trouble with my friends or my own side of the family. It's just her.\n\nDoctor: Besides your sister-in-law, do you also tend to distrust other people’s motives in general?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't think so. I mean, I've never been the type to think everyone is out to get me. I've always taken people at their word, for the most part. Like the mailman, Gary. He's been delivering our mail for fifteen years, maybe more. He always makes sure to put the packages right inside the screen door so they don't get wet if it rains. We had this awful storm last spring, the wind was howling so loud it sounded like a freight train coming right through the living room. It knocked down that big oak tree in the Johnsons' yard across the street. Took out their whole front fence and crushed their birdbath. It was a beautiful birdbath, too, made of real stone, not that cheap concrete they sell at the garden center now. I used to love watching the robins splash around in it in the mornings while I drank my coffee. I only drink one cup a day now, though, doctor said it was better for my stomach. But anyway... no, I don't distrust people in general. I just know what I see with her.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought that your husband might be involved in this too?\n\nPatient: No, no, definitely not my husband. He's a good man, he really is. We've been married for almost thirty years now. We met at a county fair, actually. He was working one of those games where you try to knock down the milk bottles with a baseball. You know the ones? They always rig them so the bottom bottles are heavier, it's almost impossible to win the giant stuffed bear. But he let me win a little blue dog anyway. I still have it, sitting on the dresser in the spare room. We painted that room a soft yellow last year... well, he painted it. I picked out the color, 'Morning Sunshine' it was called. It took three coats to cover up the awful green that was there before. We bought the paint at that hardware store downtown, the one with the creaky wooden floors. I love the smell of that place, sawdust and fertilizer. The owner, old Mr. Higgins, he just retired last month. His son took over, but it's not the same. The son doesn't know anything about plumbing fixtures... but no, my husband doesn't have anything to do with what she's doing. He just doesn't see it like I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things that other people could not see?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I mean, my eyesight isn't what it used to be, I'll admit that. I had to get bifocals last year. I fought it for the longest time, I really did. I thought they would make me look old, you know? Like my Aunt Clara. She had those thick, heavy frames with the little chain attached so she wouldn't lose them. She was always losing things, though. Once she lost her false teeth at a church picnic. We spent an hour looking for them, and it turned out she had wrapped them in a napkin and put them in her purse next to a half-eaten deviled egg. Deviled eggs always give me heartburn now, which is a shame because I make a wonderful deviled egg. The secret is just a tiny pinch of dry mustard and a splash of pickle juice. Sweet pickle juice, not dill. Dill is too sharp. I tried making dill pickles once, grew the cucumbers myself on a trellis by the back fence. But the rabbits kept getting to them before they were big enough to pick. Rabbits are cute, but they're a menace to a garden... but no, I don't see things that aren't there. My eyes are fine, except for needing the glasses for reading.\n\nDoctor: Do you generally prefer to be alone rather than spend time with other people?\n\nPatient: Oh, I wouldn't say I prefer to be alone. I like having company just fine. I used to host a bunco night with some ladies from the neighborhood a few years back. We'd take turns hosting, and whenever it was my turn, I'd make these little cucumber sandwiches. You have to cut the crusts off, or they just don't look right on the platter. I had this beautiful silver platter that belonged to my mother. She got it as a wedding gift, and she kept it polished so bright you could see your face in it. She used this special pink polish that came in a little tin. I can't find that brand anywhere anymore, I've looked all over. I even asked the boy at the grocery store last week, the one with the curly hair who always bags the groceries too heavy. He put all my canned peaches in one paper bag, and the bottom ripped right before I got to the car. Peaches rolling all over the asphalt. Luckily they were in cans, not glass jars, or it would have been a terrible, sticky mess. I used to can my own peaches, actually, back when we had a tree in the yard. But no, I don't mind being around people at all. I just like things peaceful, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you actually want close relationships with other people, or are you generally content keeping to yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, I do want close relationships, I really do. I've always been a friendly person. I mean, I used to have coffee with my neighbor, Mrs. Gable, every Tuesday morning. She lived in the blue house at the end of the cul-de-sac. It was a lovely shade of blue, kind of a robin's egg color. She had this beautiful rose garden in her front yard, the kind with those big, showy tea roses. I tried growing roses once, but the Japanese beetles just decimated them. They are awful little bugs, so shiny and green, but they just eat everything in sight. I bought this special spray from the garden center, but I think it just made them mad. The man at the garden center, he had this booming voice, you could hear him from the parking lot. He told me I needed to put out these traps, little bags that hang from a stand. But then I read somewhere that the traps actually attract *more* beetles to your yard, so I threw them all in the trash. The trash men come so early on our street, sometimes before the sun is even up. It wakes me up, the sound of that big truck grinding... but yes, I like having people close to me. I just don't have as many friends as I used to, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever, at any point earlier in your life, had similar suspicions that people were trying to use, deceive, or harm you?\n\nPatient: No, not like this. I mean, people have always been a little selfish, haven't they? That's just human nature. Like my sister-in-law, Brenda. Years ago, she borrowed my good crockpot for a church potluck and she didn't return it for three months. And when she finally did, the ceramic insert had a chip right on the rim. She swore it was there before, but I know my own kitchenware. I take very good care of my things. I always hand-wash the insert, never put it in the dishwasher, because the dishwasher detergent can be so harsh. It leaves that chalky residue on the glasses, too. I started using that rinse aid, the blue liquid in the little bottle, and it helped some, but you have to remember to refill the little compartment in the door. The door on my dishwasher is getting kind of squeaky, actually. I probably need to spray some WD-40 on the hinges. WD-40 fixes just about everything, doesn't it? My father used to keep a can of it in his glove compartment, right next to his road flares and this old, folded-up paper map. People don't use paper maps anymore, with the phones doing all the navigating. But I always liked the maps, tracing the lines with your finger... but no, I've never felt like people were plotting against me or trying to ruin me before. Just the usual thoughtlessness, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you think this kind of suspiciousness shows up in other parts of your life too, or is it only about your sister-in-law?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't think so. It's really just Brenda. I mean, I'm a trusting person, usually. I trust the mailman to bring the mail, don't I? Although, lately, he's been coming much later in the afternoon. Used to be he'd come right at 11:30, regular as clockwork. I could set my watch by it. Now sometimes it's past 3:00 before I hear the little squeak of the mailbox hinge. Our mailbox is one of those old metal ones, shaped like a little barn. My husband painted it red when we first moved in, but the sun has faded it to a sort of dusty pink. I was thinking of repainting it, maybe a nice hunter green. Green is such a calming color, don't you think? Like the forest. I went camping in the forest once when I was a teenager, up near the state park. We roasted marshmallows and I burned my tongue because I didn't wait for it to cool down. It blistered right up. But anyway, no, I don't go around suspecting everyone. Just Brenda, because of... well, you know how she is.\n\nDoctor: Have you usually been this suspicious or mistrustful of other people for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not at all. I've always been pretty open with people, ever since I was young. I started working at the diner over on Route 9 when I was sixteen—I actually left high school early because the tips were so good, and I just trusted folks to leave a fair amount on the table. We had these little glass sugar dispensers at every booth, the kind with the little metal flap on the lid? You had to unscrew them every single night and wipe them out because the sugar would get all clumpy if it was humid. The humidity here in the summer is just dreadful, isn't it? It makes my hair frizz up terribly. I bought this special smoothing serum from the drugstore, it comes in a little purple pump bottle, but it just made my hair look greasy instead of smooth. My mother always used to say a woman's hair is her crowning glory, but she wore a wig for the last twenty years of her life. It was a very stiff wig, sort of an auburn color, and she kept it on a styrofoam head on her dresser... but, oh, what were we talking about? Right, being mistrustful. No, I've never been that way. It's really just been recently, with Brenda.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this kind of mistrust toward people other than your sister-in-law?\n\nPatient: No, like I said, it's just Brenda. I don't have any reason to doubt anyone else. Well, except maybe the guy at the auto shop, but that's just because mechanics always try to upcharge you for things you don't need, like air filters. He showed me this dirty filter last time I went in for an oil change, but I'm pretty sure he just keeps one dirty filter in the back to show all the ladies who come in by themselves. My husband used to do all the oil changes in the driveway. He had these heavy metal ramps he'd drive the car up onto. They were so heavy, I could barely lift one end of them. They got rust spots all over them because he left them out in the rain one spring. It rained for almost two weeks straight that year, my tomato plants completely drowned in the garden. I had planted these beautiful heirloom tomatoes, the big purple ones? They're so sweet if you slice them up with a little mozzarella and balsamic vinegar. I buy the balsamic glaze, the thick kind in the squeeze bottle, it's so much better than the thin stuff... but anyway, no, I don't mistrust people generally. Just Brenda.\n\nDoctor: Has Brenda ever actually done anything concrete that you could point to as evidence that she is trying to control the money, or is it mainly a strong feeling you have about her intentions?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not just a feeling, I know what I've been seeing for more than a year now. She's been working this family business so she can get control of the money and push me out of decisions. I mean, they all try to smooth it over, but I'm not blind. It started right around the time we had that big ice storm last winter. Do you remember that storm? The power was out for three whole days. We had to dig out the old kerosene heater from the basement, and it smelled awful, just gave me the worst headache. I tried opening a window a crack to let some fresh air in, but it was so freezing cold the draft just went right through my bones. I had to wear three pairs of socks, those thick wool ones from the farm supply store. They're so scratchy, though, I always have to wear a thin cotton pair underneath or my ankles break out in a rash. I get contact dermatitis really easily, even from certain laundry detergents. I can only use the clear, unscented kind now. If I use anything with a fragrance, I'm itching for a week. But yes, no matter what anyone says, I know exactly what Brenda is up to with the business and the money.\n\nDoctor: Besides your sister-in-law, do you ever suspect that other people are trying to use or deceive you?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I really don't. Like I said, it's just Brenda who has it out for me with the money. Most people are perfectly fine. I mean, my neighbor across the street, Mrs. Higgins, she's a sweetheart. She brought over a casserole last week when I was feeling a bit under the weather. It was a tuna noodle casserole, the kind with the crushed potato chips on top? I haven't had that since I was a little girl. My grandmother used to make it every Friday during Lent because we couldn't eat meat. She'd use those really wide egg noodles, and she always put peas in it, which I hated. I used to pick the peas out and hide them under my napkin. Once, my grandfather caught me doing it, but he just winked and didn't say a word. He was a good man, worked at the lumber mill for forty years until he lost two fingers in a bandsaw accident. He had to learn how to hold his coffee mug with his left hand after that... but no, nobody else is trying to use me or anything like that. Just Brenda.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least a week where you felt unusually high, very energetic, or needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't ever felt like that. I sleep just fine, usually. Well, mostly. Sometimes I wake up around three in the morning because the neighbor's dog barks. It's a little terrier mix, scruffy looking thing, and they leave him out on the patio all night. I don't know why people get dogs if they're just going to leave them outside in the dark. It gets so chilly out there, especially in October. I bought this heavy quilt at a craft fair last October, actually. It has these beautiful patchwork squares, all done in autumn colors—burnt orange, mustard yellow, a really deep burgundy. The lady who made it said it took her six months to stitch by hand. She was from two towns over, said she learned to quilt from her mother when she was just a little girl. I used to sew a bit, mostly just mending things, hemming pants. My husband was always tearing the knees out of his work jeans. I bought these iron-on patches once, but they never stayed on after you washed them. You'd pull them out of the dryer and the patch would just be flapping by one corner... but no, I've never had a week where I felt high or didn't need sleep.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Well, I... I just don't see how there could be any proof of that, because I know what I've been seeing for more than a year now. I came here because my husband's sister has been working this family business so she can get control of the money and push me out of decisions. They all try to smooth it over, but I know the truth. It's like when they try to tell you a used car runs perfectly. My husband bought this old Chevy truck back in the nineties. The salesman swore up and down it had just been serviced, had a brand new transmission. We didn't even make it halfway home before it started making this awful grinding noise, like metal chewing on metal. We had to pull over on the shoulder of Route 9, right by that diner that used to sell those giant cherry pies. Oh, they were so good. The crust was always perfectly flaky. I tried to make a cherry pie once from scratch, pitting all those cherries by hand. My fingers were stained red for three days! I looked like I'd been in a crime scene. I tried washing my hands with bleach, lemon juice, everything. The lemon juice just stung because I had a paper cut on my thumb from opening the mail. We get so much junk mail these days, mostly catalogs for things I'd never buy. Who needs a battery-operated pepper grinder anyway? Just twist it yourself! But no, no matter what kind of proof someone tried to show me, I know exactly what Brenda is doing.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F60.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m Dr. ____. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Nice to meet you, Doctor. I came in because... well, it's my husband’s sister. She has been working this family business so she can get control of the money and push me out of all the decisions. And she thinks I don't notice! Just like last week, she came over bringing these cheap store-bought muffins. Who brings store-bought muffins when they know I bake? Like I was just saying, I know what I’ve been seeing for more than a year now. No matter how much folks try to smooth it over and tell me I'm just misunderstanding her, I know she's working this family business just so she can get control of the money and push me out of the decisions. \n\nIt's all about the money with her. It's like a lullaby, really. Speaking of lullabies, I used to sing the sweetest little songs to my nieces when they were babies. They're all grown up now, of course, off to college and getting married. One of them just had a baby of her own, a little boy. They named him Oliver. Such an old-fashioned name, don't you think? I always liked the name Michael, myself. If I'd had a boy, I would have named him Michael. But we never had children. Just a house full of cats. We have three right now: Fluffy, Whiskers, and Barnaby. Barnaby is a terror, always knocking things off the counter. Just yesterday he broke my favorite coffee mug, the one I got in Florida... but no, no voices in my head, if that's what you mean. Just the cats meowing for their dinner.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any experiences of people sending you special messages or signs meant just for you?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't seen anything like that. Just regular things. Well, except for the mailman, he always leaves the letters kind of crooked in the box. I keep meaning to ask him about it, but he comes by so early. We used to have a mailman named Gary, oh, he was just wonderful. He'd always stop and give Barnaby a little scratch behind the ears if he was sitting on the porch. Gary retired last year, moved down to Arizona, I think. Or maybe it was New Mexico. Somewhere hot and dry. I couldn't live somewhere without trees. The changing of the leaves in the fall is my favorite time of year. We have this big oak tree in the front yard, and the acorns... oh my goodness, the acorns. Raking them up is a full-time job. My husband says we should just cut the tree down, but I couldn't bear it. It provides so much shade in the summer. But no, no special signs or anything like that. Just regular mail, mostly bills and those coupon flyers.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it is possible that your sister-in-law is not trying to control the money or push you out?\n\nPatient: No, absolutely not. I know what I've been seeing for over a year now. She's working that family business just so she can get her hands on all the money and push me right out of any decisions. My husband tries to smooth it over, says I'm just getting worked up over nothing, but I know better. He's always been like that, though, trying to keep the peace. Even when we were dating, he'd never send food back at a restaurant, even if it was stone cold. We used to go to this little diner on Main Street, they had the best cherry pie. Real cherries, not that canned filling stuff. The waitress there, Betty, she worked there for thirty years before they closed it down to build that new pharmacy. I don't know why we need another pharmacy, there's one on every corner now. And the prices... I went in just to get some aspirin and couldn't believe what they were charging. I ended up just going to the grocery store instead. The grocery store is always so crowded on Tuesdays, though, because of the senior discount. I'm not quite old enough for it yet, but my neighbor Mildred is. She always goes on Tuesdays and brings back those little bakery cookies... but no, I'm not wrong about my sister-in-law. I know exactly what she's doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way about other people being out to get you for as long as you can remember, even back in your younger adult years?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. It's just her, really. I never used to worry about people like this when I was younger. Back then, I was too busy working at the shoe factory. Oh, I worked there for twelve years before it shut down. We made the most beautiful leather boots. Real leather, you know, the kind that smells so rich when you open the box. I still have a pair I bought with my employee discount in 1990. They don't make shoes like that anymore, everything is plastic and falls apart after one season. It's a shame, really. The man who owned the factory, Mr. Henderson, he was such a fair boss. Every Christmas he'd give us all a turkey and a tin of those Danish butter cookies. The ones in the blue tin? I always ended up using the tin to store my sewing supplies. I used to sew a lot of my own clothes back then. Patterns were so cheap at the fabric store... but no, it's just my sister-in-law I have to keep an eye on now. I've always gotten along fine with everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally been suspicious of close friends or family members for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I've always been a pretty trusting person, really. I mean, my friends and I, we've known each other for years. My friend Susan, we met back when our kids were in kindergarten together. Her son, Tommy, he was the sweetest little boy, always had these bright red overalls on. They lived just two streets over, in that yellow house with the big wrap-around porch. I always wanted a porch like that. We used to sit out there in the summer and drink iced tea... real iced tea, not that powdered mix stuff. You have to boil the water and steep the bags just right, and add a sprig of mint if you have it. I tried growing mint once, but it just took over the whole garden. It choked out all my tomatoes. I was so upset, I had been planning on canning those tomatoes. My grandmother taught me how to can when I was just a little girl. We'd spend all day in the hot kitchen, putting up peaches and green beans... but no, I've never had any trouble with my friends or my own side of the family. It's just her.\n\nDoctor: Besides your sister-in-law, do you also tend to distrust other people’s motives in general?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't think so. I mean, I've never been the type to think everyone is out to get me. I've always taken people at their word, for the most part. Like the mailman, Gary. He's been delivering our mail for fifteen years, maybe more. He always makes sure to put the packages right inside the screen door so they don't get wet if it rains. We had this awful storm last spring, the wind was howling so loud it sounded like a freight train coming right through the living room. It knocked down that big oak tree in the Johnsons' yard across the street. Took out their whole front fence and crushed their birdbath. It was a beautiful birdbath, too, made of real stone, not that cheap concrete they sell at the garden center now. I used to love watching the robins splash around in it in the mornings while I drank my coffee. I only drink one cup a day now, though, doctor said it was better for my stomach. But anyway... no, I don't distrust people in general. I just know what I see with her.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought that your husband might be involved in this too?\n\nPatient: No, no, definitely not my husband. He's a good man, he really is. We've been married for almost thirty years now. We met at a county fair, actually. He was working one of those games where you try to knock down the milk bottles with a baseball. You know the ones? They always rig them so the bottom bottles are heavier, it's almost impossible to win the giant stuffed bear. But he let me win a little blue dog anyway. I still have it, sitting on the dresser in the spare room. We painted that room a soft yellow last year... well, he painted it. I picked out the color, 'Morning Sunshine' it was called. It took three coats to cover up the awful green that was there before. We bought the paint at that hardware store downtown, the one with the creaky wooden floors. I love the smell of that place, sawdust and fertilizer. The owner, old Mr. Higgins, he just retired last month. His son took over, but it's not the same. The son doesn't know anything about plumbing fixtures... but no, my husband doesn't have anything to do with what she's doing. He just doesn't see it like I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen things that other people could not see?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I mean, my eyesight isn't what it used to be, I'll admit that. I had to get bifocals last year. I fought it for the longest time, I really did. I thought they would make me look old, you know? Like my Aunt Clara. She had those thick, heavy frames with the little chain attached so she wouldn't lose them. She was always losing things, though. Once she lost her false teeth at a church picnic. We spent an hour looking for them, and it turned out she had wrapped them in a napkin and put them in her purse next to a half-eaten deviled egg. Deviled eggs always give me heartburn now, which is a shame because I make a wonderful deviled egg. The secret is just a tiny pinch of dry mustard and a splash of pickle juice. Sweet pickle juice, not dill. Dill is too sharp. I tried making dill pickles once, grew the cucumbers myself on a trellis by the back fence. But the rabbits kept getting to them before they were big enough to pick. Rabbits are cute, but they're a menace to a garden... but no, I don't see things that aren't there. My eyes are fine, except for needing the glasses for reading.\n\nDoctor: Do you generally prefer to be alone rather than spend time with other people?\n\nPatient: Oh, I wouldn't say I prefer to be alone. I like having company just fine. I used to host a bunco night with some ladies from the neighborhood a few years back. We'd take turns hosting, and whenever it was my turn, I'd make these little cucumber sandwiches. You have to cut the crusts off, or they just don't look right on the platter. I had this beautiful silver platter that belonged to my mother. She got it as a wedding gift, and she kept it polished so bright you could see your face in it. She used this special pink polish that came in a little tin. I can't find that brand anywhere anymore, I've looked all over. I even asked the boy at the grocery store last week, the one with the curly hair who always bags the groceries too heavy. He put all my canned peaches in one paper bag, and the bottom ripped right before I got to the car. Peaches rolling all over the asphalt. Luckily they were in cans, not glass jars, or it would have been a terrible, sticky mess. I used to can my own peaches, actually, back when we had a tree in the yard. But no, I don't mind being around people at all. I just like things peaceful, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you actually want close relationships with other people, or are you generally content keeping to yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, I do want close relationships, I really do. I've always been a friendly person. I mean, I used to have coffee with my neighbor, Mrs. Gable, every Tuesday morning. She lived in the blue house at the end of the cul-de-sac. It was a lovely shade of blue, kind of a robin's egg color. She had this beautiful rose garden in her front yard, the kind with those big, showy tea roses. I tried growing roses once, but the Japanese beetles just decimated them. They are awful little bugs, so shiny and green, but they just eat everything in sight. I bought this special spray from the garden center, but I think it just made them mad. The man at the garden center, he had this booming voice, you could hear him from the parking lot. He told me I needed to put out these traps, little bags that hang from a stand. But then I read somewhere that the traps actually attract *more* beetles to your yard, so I threw them all in the trash. The trash men come so early on our street, sometimes before the sun is even up. It wakes me up, the sound of that big truck grinding... but yes, I like having people close to me. I just don't have as many friends as I used to, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever, at any point earlier in your life, had similar suspicions that people were trying to use, deceive, or harm you?\n\nPatient: No, not like this.
